TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

Nowhere is that truer than in Ukraine, which alleged this week that the billionaire’s company SpaceX had restricted some access to its Starlink satellite internet service—capping the speed at which Ukrainian weapons can remain connected to the internet in Kyiv’s ongoing war against Russia. Musk claimed he wasn’t behind the restrictions, saying in a post on X that Ukraine’s military uses a military-grade version of Starlink called Starshield that is overseen by the U.S. government and that any decision to impose restrictions would have come from the Trump administration, not him. U.S. President Donald Trump has been pressuring Ukraine to stop strikes on Russia’s energy sector, as high gas prices cast a shadow over the upcoming U.S. midterm election. SpaceX and the Pentagon did not immediately respond to requests for comment from SitRep. But the Ukraine brouhaha served as an acute reminder of how closely interlinked Musk and his businesses are with the U.S. government and how dependent multiple countries are on the whims of the world’s richest man. As Rishi has previously written , Starlink has been indispensable for the Ukrainian military and people, allowing the country to stay connected and in the fight since the early days of the war. But Musk has also sometimes expressed sympathy for Russia’s perspective and echoed Kremlin talking points. He did so again on Wednesday, saying Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky must “go” in response to Zelensky’s statement that he would not end the war until Russia was defeated. “Even if you’re not 100 percent sure whether this was Elon Musk’s decision or whether it was the U.S. government’s decision, do you want to become dependent on a company whose head is inserting himself in your conflict?” Sharon Weinberger, the au

In a month littered with dark spots for Ukraine, a bigger, slower-moving storm cloud is drifting toward the embattled nation: the chance of a budget shortfall of tens of billions of dollars if Kyiv fails to pass reforms needed for continued European financial support. Ultimately, experts believe that Ukraine will likely still be able to count on major European backing. But the saga of the budget gap, as well as tough economic conditions for 2027 and beyond, suggests that Ukraine will increasingly have to walk a fine line to maintain funding for its defense as the war stretches into a fifth year. “I think substantial European support will continue, but we should not think that any future programs are definitely secured or guaranteed,” said Sergiy Tsivkach, former head of the national investment agency UkraineInvest. Ukraine’s budget has long been under pressure: The country’s gross domestic product contracted by nearly 30 percent in 2022, the first year of Russia’s invasion, even as spending on military equipment and salaries rose 640 percent . With financial support from the United States, Europe, and others, though, Ukraine squeaked through without too much concern each year—until now. In August, Ukraine’s officials announced they urgently needed $27 billion more for their defense budget , reportedly causing shock among officials from Europe, which has supplanted the U.S. as Ukraine’s biggest financial supporter. Ukrainian officials said the budget gap was partly the result of the decision to increase military salaries, as well as the cost of drone procurement. Ukraine in September reduced the gap to $20 billion in part through austerity measures. Nepal was already confronting the growing challenges of preparing for disasters that are becoming more extreme and difficult to predict and responding to them with fewer resources. But after catastrophic flooding hit the country twice since August, another missing piece has been exposed: the absence of the U.S. Agency for International Development (USAID), once a steadfast partner in Nepal’s disaster risk reduction and humanitarian response. Development workers say that the U.S. response to the flooding along the Bhotekoshi and Trishuli rivers has been limited compared to past disasters, including the devastating 2015 earthquake, when USAID worked with multiple organizations in rescue and relief operations and served, in one advisor’s words, as the “lead U.S. government agency for coordinating foreign disaster relief.” Nepal was already confronting the growing challenges of preparing for disasters that are becoming more extreme and difficult to predict and responding to them with fewer resources. But after catastrophic flooding hit the country twice since August, another missing piece has been exposed: the absence of the U.S. Agency for International Development (USAID), once a steadfast partner in Nepal’s disaster risk reduction and humanitarian response. Development workers say that the U.S. response to the flooding along the Bhotekoshi and Trishuli rivers has been limited compared to past disasters, including the devastating 2015 earthquake, when USAID worked with multiple organizations in rescue and relief operations and served, in one advisor’s words, as the “lead U.S. government agency for coordinating foreign disaster relief.” “Compared to the 2015 response, the difference is night and day,” Santosh Gyawali—the former Nepal representative for USAID’s Bureau for Humanitarian Assistance, which oversaw disaster response coordination—told Foreign Policy . “The U.S. Disaster Assistance Response Team arrived for search-and-rescue operations and did quick assessments on building safety and saved lives. They worked as auxiliary to the Nepal government. We see that missing now,” Gyawali added. USAID was effectively destroyed in July 2025 following months of funding freezes and mass layoffs conducted under the Trump administration’s Department of Government Efficiency.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/15/nepal-usaid-doge-floods-united-states-funding/

* Algeria’s New Bid for Regional Influence
  Foreign Policy | 15 Oct 15:38 UTC
  A sharp break with the UAE underscores the North African country’s ambitions to reassert its presence on the international stage after decades on the periphery.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Years of simmering tensions between Algiers and Abu Dhabi boiled over last month when Algeria’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs announced that the country was severing diplomatic relations with the United Arab Emirates. The ministry did not cite a specific reason for the split, but the decision appeared to stem from Algeria’s frustration over Emirati support for armed groups in North Africa—including possibly inside Algeria—which Algiers considers destabilizing. This diplomatic rupture reflects the increasingly assertive foreign policy Algeria’s current leadership has advanced since coming to power in 2019. Algiers long prided itself on being a mediator in the region but saw that role decline in recent decades on account of the country’s 1992-2002 civil war and the declining health of former President Abdelaziz Bouteflika. Now, under President Abdelmadjid Tebboune, things are changing: By pursuing foreign partnerships, recalibrating its relations with Washington and Moscow, and taking a decisive stance against Abu Dhabi, Algiers is demonstrating its intention to reclaim its regional and international influence after decades of absence. Now, under President Abdelmadjid Tebboune, things are changing: By pursuing foreign partnerships, recalibrating its relations with Washington and Moscow, and taking a decisive stance against Abu Dhabi, Algiers is demonstrating its intention to reclaim its regional and international influence after decades of absence. * Why Spain Is Standing Up to Trump
  Foreign Policy | 15 Oct 14:52 UTC
  Foreign Minister Albares: “We believe in international law.”
  FULL TEXT: No audio? Hover over the video player, and tap the Click to Unmute button. Subtitles are also available. Closed captioning provided by Vimeo and may contain minor inconsistencies. Register now to watch this on-demand recording. Only FP subscribers can submit questions for FP Live interviews. Only FP subscribers can submit questions for FP Live interviews. Countries around the world employ a variety of strategies to navigate the Trump White House. Some try flattery and appeasement. Others keep a low profile. Spain has chosen a path of frank disagreement. Under Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez, Madrid has refused to follow Washington’s call for NATO members to increase defense spending to 5 percent of GDP and has rejected President Donald Trump’s request to use Spanish bases in the war with Iran. On issue after issue, from immigration to the Israeli-Palestinian dispute, Spain’s left-wing government has publicly forged a path that runs counter to a global trend of right-wing policies. But now Sánchez’s government faces elections in November, and Spaniards will decide whether the country’s foreign and domestic policy is something they see as reasonable. * The Radioactive Ore That Could Break China’s Grip on Rare Earths
  Foreign Policy | 15 Oct 13:00 UTC
  Western powers are scrambling for monazite ore—but can they handle it?
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. This article is a collaboration between IEEE Spectrum , the flagship magazine of the Institute of Electrical and Electronics Engineers, and Foreign Policy . The global campaigns to break China’s dominance of vital rare-earth markets are shaping up into one of the signature geostrategic struggles of the early 21st century. Hundreds of mining companies, government agencies, and industrial facilities scattered across North America, Australasia, and Europe are striving to compete with Chinese firms, which control roughly 90 percent of the markets for rare-earth oxides and rare-earth metals. This article is a collaboration between IEEE Spectrum , the flagship magazine of the Institute of Electrical and Electronics Engineers, and Foreign Policy . The global campaigns to break China’s dominance of vital rare-earth markets are shaping up into one of the signature geostrategic struggles of the early 21st century. Hundreds of mining companies, government agencies, and industrial facilities scattered across North America, Australasia, and Europe are striving to compete with Chinese firms, which control roughly 90 percent of the markets for rare-earth oxides and rare-earth metals. These are the ingredients upon which vast and crucial manufacturing categories depend: semiconductors , LEDs , lasers, displays, metal alloys, optical lenses, and catalysts for petroleum and chemical processing. Most important, though, are permanent magnets. Essentially all powerful electric motors (and most high-quality audio speakers) are based on rare-earth permanent magnets. These motors are the heart of electric vehicles, heating and cooling systems, robots, appliances, tools, and military systems—from tactical drones to ballistic-missile submarines. Succinctly put, any industrial power that wants to remain competitive in vital, high-tech industries must have access to a steady supply of rare-earth elements or products based on them. The traditional supply chain for these elements, originating in China, is increasingly fraught—so much so that some analysts are beginning to use the term “ mineral war .” But the countries that are now scrambling to piece together alternative supply chains, whether by themselves or in cooperation with each other, face daunting odds. Putting aside the sheer momentum of the Chinese rare-earths juggernaut, few industries can match the rare-earths business for instability, risk, intrigue, and sheer frustration. A work
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/15/monazite-china-rare-earths-radioactive/

* The West Needs a Pax Biologica
  Foreign Policy | 15 Oct 09:00 UTC
  Chinese firms are displacing Western competitors in one of the century’s most important and most overlooked industries.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Every U.S. politician wants to reinvigorate manufacturing, but most are chasing a 20th-century version of it. Visions of blue-collar Americans assembling cars or forging steel might have electoral appeal, but they do not reflect the industries that will meaningfully bolster the U.S. economy and its competitiveness. High-tech manufacturing of semiconductors and other parts of the artificial intelligence supply chain have received plenty of attention, but these products make up only a narrow slice of the physical economy. To truly revitalize the United States’ manufacturing ability, it needs a new paradigm that can touch all corners of the economy. Every U.S. politician wants to reinvigorate manufacturing, but most are chasing a 20th-century version of it. Visions of blue-collar Americans assembling cars or forging steel might have electoral appeal, but they do not reflect the industries that will meaningfully bolster the U.S. economy and its competitiveness. High-tech manufacturing of semiconductors and other parts of the artificial intelligence supply chain have received plenty of attention, but these products make up only a narrow slice of the physical economy. To truly revitalize the United States’ manufacturing ability, it needs a new paradigm that can touch all corners of the economy. Simply put, biomanufacturing entails using a biological system, usually a living microbe or a specialized protein, to make goods. For millennia, humans have used biomanufacturing, from the fermentation used to produce beer or cheese to the scaling of penicillin production during World War II, and, more recently, the development of recombinant human insulin in the 1970s. Today, biomanufacturing can produce everything from mRNA vaccines to materials used to make plastics . Soon, a wide range of advanced materials, bulk chemicals, and even cement could be grown in specialized vats and bags using methods that are more precise, resource-efficient, and environmentally friendly than traditional manufacturing. The way we produce agricultural goods, materials, consumer products, and more—as much as 60 percent of the world’s physical inputs—could be transformed by biomanufacturing in the coming decades, a market shift worth trillions of dollars. The United States, as the pioneer of this industry, still holds a slight lead , particularly across the laboratory tools, vats, specialized consumables, and other technologies that underlie biomanu
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/15/china-us-biomanufacturing-competition-industrial-policy/

* COP17: Tracking progress towards the UN’s 2030 nature targets
  Carbon Brief | 15 Oct 16:07 UTC
  Countries will meet in Armenia this month to carry out the first global review of progress towards meeting key nature… The post COP17: Tracking progress towards the UN’s 2030 nature targets appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Countries will meet in Armenia this month to carry out the first global review of progress towards meeting key nature targets by 2030. These targets – and the wider ambition to halt and reverse biodiversity loss around the world – were agreed in the landmark Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework in 2022 . But the ambitions will not be achieved in time for the 2030 deadline unless action “accelerates rapidly”, a key UN report found. It noted that nations are falling short on 22 of 23 nature targets, which focus on restoring ecosystems, increasing nature finance and reducing harmful subsidies. This report and other findings will inform the global progress review taking place at the COP17 biodiversity summit in Armenia over 19-30 October. The sections below summarise the report’s findings on where progress has – and has not – been made on each of the 23 targets. They also rank the extent to which countries have addressed elements of each target on a national basis. Click on each target for more information. Key terms are defined throughout each section and in a glossary below. “Ensure that all areas are under participatory, integrated and biodiversity inclusive spatial planning and/or effective management processes…to bring the loss of areas of high biodiversity importance…close to zero by 2030, while respecting the rights of Indigenous peoples and local communities.” “Ensure that by 2030 at least 30% of areas of degraded terrestrial, inland water and marine and coastal ecosystems are under effective restoration…” “Ensure and enable that by 2030 at least 30% of terrestrial and inland water areas and of marine and coastal areas…are effectively conserved and managed…and integrated into wider landscapes, seascapes and the ocean, while ensuring that any sustainable use…is fully consistent with conservation outcomes, recognising and respecting the rights of Indigenous peoples and local communities, including over their traditional territories.” “Ensure urgent management actions to halt human-induced extinction of known threatened species and for the recovery and conservation of species, in particular threatened species, to significantly reduce extinction risk…and effectively manage human-wildlife interactions to minimise human-wildlife conflict.” “Ensure that the use, harvesting and trade of wild species is sustainable, safe and legal…while respecting and protecting customary sustainable use by Indigenous peoples and local communities.” “Eliminate, minimi
  https://interactive.carbonbrief.org/nature-targets-2030-progress/

* COP17: Developed countries ‘fall far short’ of UN targets for nature finance
  Carbon Brief | 15 Oct 15:40 UTC
  The amount of finance for nature delivered to date “falls far short” of what is needed to meet the UN’s… The post COP17: Developed countries ‘fall far short’ of UN targets for nature finance appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: The amount of finance for nature delivered to date “falls far short” of what is needed to meet the UN’s global goals, according to a report released ahead of the COP17 biodiversity summit. The global stocktaking report , prepared by the UN Convention on Biological Diversity (UN CBD) ahead of this month’s summit in Armenia, finds that developed nations are failing to close the $200bn “biodiversity finance gap”. Initiatives designed to support this goal are also stumbling, with a special “framework” fund for biodiversity receiving less than $140m from developed countries in the past two years and the “ landmark ” Cali Fund receiving only $6,000 from private companies. The availability of finance – or lack thereof – is likely to be a key point of contention in negotiations at COP17 in Yerevan, Armenia, which begin on 19 October. At COP15 in 2022, 196 countries agreed to an ambitious global deal to reverse biodiversity loss by 2030, dubbed the Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework (GBF). Goal D of the agreement, shown below, states that countries must “close” a $700bn “biodiversity finance gap” by 2030. This overall $700bn is made up of two parts: changes to nature-harming subsidies ; and raising financial resources to support biodiversity. Target 18 within the text calls for countries to “identify by 2025, and eliminate, phase out or reform incentives, including subsidies, harmful for biodiversity…progressively reducing them by at least $500bn per year by 2030”. The remaining $200bn should be met by “mobilising” resources, according to target 19 of the GBF. It states that countries should “substantially and progressively increase the level of financial resources from all sources” to “at least” $200bn per year by 2030. The overarching $200bn can be from national budgets, multilateral development banks or money “mobilised” from the private sector. However, the target also specifically states that “at least $20bn per year by 2025 and…at least $30bn per year by 2030” should be public biodiversity funding provided to developing countries by developed countries – or others that “voluntarily assume” their obligations. According to the global stocktaking report prepared by the CBD, between 2020 and 2023, countries mobilised a cumulative total of just $186.4bn. This is equivalent to an average of less than $50bn per year. While the report – published on 10 September 2026 – cautions that it is “still too early to draw any conclusions”, it adds that this total 
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/cop17-developed-countries-fall-far-short-of-un-targets-for-nature-finance

* If Taiz Falls, Yemen May Never Be the Same
  New Lines | 15 Oct 15:42 UTC
  Yemen’s southwestern Taiz governorate has been a vital center of resistance to the Houthis for over a decade. If the group consolidates its hold on the area and takes the city of Taiz itself, the effects for the country could be lasting and profound. The post If Taiz Falls, Yemen May Never Be the Same appeared first on New Lines Magazine ...
  FULL TEXT: Taiz governorate in southwestern Yemen was never simply another front in the country’s war. The seven-day lightning offensive in September — in which the Houthis seized the strategic port city of Mokha and the Bab al-Mandab Strait between the Red Sea and the Gulf of Aden — amounted to more than a change on a map. And several districts in the south, west and east of Taiz governorate collapsed between Sept. 13 and Oct. 10, extending Houthi control to additional strategic areas and consolidating their gains on Yemen’s western coast. Taiz governorate has been one of the most important centers of resistance to the Houthis throughout the conflict. Its demographic, political and historical weight means that battles there have an importance that extends far beyond the governorate itself. If the Houthis consolidate their hold on what remains of the countryside and the city of Taiz itself, they could reshape the balance of power in Yemen. Recent events reveal a painful paradox: Taiz was a popular base of support for the republican system, which was born in 1962 following the overthrow of the theocratic monarchy that ruled the country. The population also had combat experience. But locals failed to forge a unified military institution capable of protecting the area and honoring its sacrifices. The recent collapse was not simply the result of a shortage of fighters or a lack of will to fight; it was also the product of failures in command, coordination and supply, and the subordination of nationwide institution-building to partisan and personal interests. For roughly 12 years, Taiz governorate remained a major center of resistance, even while the Houthis controlled parts of the city of Taiz and large stretches of its countryside, including al-Hawban, the governorate’s most important economic and commercial center. But the endurance of its people and fighters never translated into a sustainable defense system. The front lines remained exposed to fragmented decision-making, competing interests and weak coordination, until the nightmare of a renewed siege returned. The question today is not only whether the Houthis will be able to take the city of Taiz, but what this development would mean for Yemen’s future. Would it represent a military setback that can be contained, or a strategic shift that could redraw the balance of power in western Yemen, weaken the anti-Houthi camp and deepen the divide between north and south? This is not only a story of retreating front lines, 
  https://newlinesmag.com/argument/if-taiz-falls-yemen-may-never-be-the-same/

  FULL TEXT: Krill, writes Gordon Peake, are “the least zeitgeisty creatures imaginable”: small, shrimp-like crustaceans whose total population numbers in the hundreds of trillions. Peake spends weeks aboard the Allankay with a crew of vegan activists devoted to ending the krill trade. His dispatch is a brisk history of extractive capitalism in the Antarctic, a fascinating study of an utterly uncool creature, and an uproarious account of a reluctant sailor earning his sea legs. Take the journey with him. I contemplated the fillets of pink salmon defrosting on my kitchen counter, not too far from the shelf where I keep my supplements, including krill oil, part of my long-established daily routine of taking elixirs with vague but impressive claims about “improved heart health” and “enhanced brain function.” Labels depicting regal seabirds and majestic trawlers and a barmy sense that the more expensive the supplement, the more potent it must be also guided my “decision-making.” I told Hammarstedt I was the sort of fellow who enabled such an industry to exist. O’Connor spends time with dendrochronologists, who study tree rings, and other experts to understand what they’re learning: Anthropologist Omer Stewart, who began documenting Native American burning practices in the nineteen-thirties . . . found that Indigenous communities burned prairies to attract bison to fresh grass and to maintain oak “orchards” where food could be gathered. He became convinced that Native Americans had reshaped the geography, and in some cases the climate, of North American ecosystems. Their impact “was profound, in terms of the amount of land converted to agriculture, the species they hunted and fished, and most particularluthe way they shaped their world through the use of fire,” the naturalist Scott Weidensaul wrote, in his 2012 book The First Frontier . Weidensaul argued that early America “was fruitful beyond modern recognition, it was riotous in its natural wealth, but it was not wilderness.” The dendrochronologist Evan Larson, at the University of Wisconsin-Platteville, made a similar claim: settlers encountered rich landscapes because Native Americans were tending them. “Whenever you’re talking about fire, you’re engaging with a central narrative around this untamed frontier and wilderness,” Larson told me. “In fact, that’s never been true. The early success of pioneers was already benefitting from the labor of somebody else.” “Nature writing after everyone stopped paying attention.” “Godwits make the longest non-stop migration in the bird world.   FULL TEXT: It was on Jan. 7, 2024, that I verified Israel’s drone strike on the car driven by Hamza al-Dahdouh, the Palestinian journalist and son of Al Jazeera’s Bureau Chief Wael al-Dahdouh, alongside his colleagues Mustafa Thuraya, Qusai Salem and Hazem Rajab. They make a stop at Al-Mughraqa and we would take an additional ride or walk to the medical school.   Why aren’t we all Neanderthals? These ancient bird figurines suggest that art was essential to our evolutionary success - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Hohle Fels cave in southern Germany has been a place of great interest to archaeologists since it was first excavated in 1870. Discoveries at the site date back as far as 43,000 years and include some of the earliest figurative art and oldest musical instruments ever unearthed. As the Hohle Fels excavation leader Nicholas Conard discusses in this short video, such discoveries point to complex Ice Age cultures built on myths, beliefs and rituals – that is to say, societies that were in many ways not so different from our own. In particular, Conard turns to the recent discovery of two tiny ivory birds at the site to explore why he believes a sophisticated symbolic culture may have given humans an evolutionary advantage over other hominins, including Neanderthals. * Beyond the Glass
  Longreads | 15 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Among a collection’s many skulls, a grieving son learns how to live in a world that keeps going.
  FULL TEXT: Daniel Barnum | Longreads | October 15, 2026 | 4,116 words (15 minutes) This essay is co-published with the literary magazine AGNI and appears in this fall’s AGNI 104 . The Hyrtl Skull Collection is the first exhibit you notice when you walk into the Mütter Museum at the College of Physicians of Philadelphia. This is by curatorial design: The skulls face the main entrance, staring steadily at each person who crosses the threshold. All 139 are evenly spaced in long lines behind the glass doors of a towering cherrywood cabinet. Each one rests in a wire frame on top of a thin balsa box with a tag at its center. I recall tracking them with glances as I shuffled nearby with two friends, as part of a large crowd. Small bright lights illuminated them from inside the cases, making them glow dully from my angle. Seeing them for the first time, I was both mesmerized by this effect and intimidated by the skulls’ great number. I tried not to look for too long, but the density of people on the narrow walkway made that impossible: I was soon stuck in the dead center of the exhibit. Almost in spite of myself, I scanned the wall text and learned how they had been brought together to disprove a piece of 19th-century race science, which seemed like one of the better reasons to collect skulls. Most of the exhibits in the Mütter Museum have similarly odd origins. Tucked into a corner of the College of Physicians’ stately New Beaux-Arts building, the Mütter is a 19th-century medical teaching collection/cabinet museum with an annual visitorship of almost 150,000 people. Its staff goes on late-night talk shows with specimens in tow, hosts YouTube series. The gift shop sells soaps and keychains in the shapes of its artifacts. Coming out of a death-obsessed past into an incongruous living present, the exhibits are caught somewhere between scientific exemplar and exploitative spectacle. The museum acknowledges its reputation as a freak show and, in response, doubles down on the meanings of curiosity. Signs we saw at the main gate declared the place’s ethos: disturbingly informative . The text beside the skulls explained itself: Brought together is a euphemistic way of saying, assembled by grave robbing . Dr. Hyrtl dug up a lot of his collection, or had assistants do it for him. Or paid other collectors, other doctors. The graves were attached to hospitals, madhouses, prisons. Some of the people these skulls belonged to surely had surviving relatives, though none could afford or car
  https://longreads.com/2026/10/15/mother-museum-skulls-grief-agni/

* What the West can learn from AI optimists in the rest of the world
  Rest of World | 15 Oct 10:00 UTC
  The developing world’s enthusiastic adoption of AI and necessity-driven innovations offer lessons for an anxiety-ridden West.
  FULL TEXT: On a recent Sunday afternoon, I was invited to debate AI’s impact on the cultural sector alongside Dutch politicians and creative-industry leaders. I was the lone optimist, arguing that if fear and risk become our primary lens for governing artificial intelligence, we will design systems around what we want to prevent rather than what we want to use them for. Then I said something that was harder for the room to hear: The Global South is home to some of AI’s most enthusiastic and inventive users, and their experiments could help us rethink our own institutions. In the West, fear of AI has helped turn pessimism into a cultural virtue. The U.S. is no different from Europe. A culture of fear encourages protectionism: We hunker down and romanticize legacy systems rather than ask what might replace them. Creative industries in the majority world have spent decades navigating piracy and weak enforcement, forcing them to think beyond rights-based policing about how creators actually receive value. Anghami , the biggest music app in the Middle East, for instance, pays musicians from their first stream, rather than only after a song reaches a minimum threshold. It has approached AI pragmatically, using it to curate, tag, and attribute creative work rather than simply blocking and banning. In the West, fear of AI has helped turn pessimism into a cultural virtue. Would you publicly say you love AI? Your answer may say something about where you’re from. Say “yes” to a concerned audience in the U.S. or the Netherlands, and you risk being seen as naive, or complicit in extraction and exploitation. While the U.S. is building and deploying AI at a scale no other country matches, Americans are deeply anxious about where it is heading. Only 17% of U.S. adults think AI will have a positive impact on the country over the next 20 years, according to a 2025 Pew Research Center survey . In much of the Global South, the mood is markedly different . Stanford’s 2025 AI Index found that 83% of respondents in China, 80% in Indonesia, and 77% in Thailand said AI-powered products offer more benefits than drawbacks, compared with 39% in the U.S. and 36% in the Netherlands. * The ladder of knowledge
  Aeon | 15 Oct 10:00 UTC
  What we claim as ‘true’ is not a ceiling we are forever failing to reach but something that changes as we climb toward it - by Alexander V Gheorghiu Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Hamburg, Germany, 1937. Photo by Herbert List/Magnum Photos On the evening of 8 September 1930, the German mathematician David Hilbert stepped up to a microphone in Königsberg and addressed his countrymen by radio. He was 68, recently retired, and at the height of his fame. The talk was a defence of pure reason, delivered in the city of Immanuel Kant, and it ended with two phrases that would later be carved on his tombstone: Wir müssen wissen. Wir werden wissen . We must know. We shall know. David Hilbert. Courtesy the University of Göttingen Are certain questions closed to the human mind? Are there truths about the world that we can never know? Hilbert thought that, at least within mathematics, this could not be the case. For him, mathematics was a game played with symbols: one could fix the right axioms as rules of the game and every mathematical problem could be solved. He was wrong. The day before his Königsberg address, at a small academic meeting in the same city, a 24-year-old Austrian named Kurt Gödel had quietly announced that no such system for arithmetic could exist. Whatever reasonable set of rules you fix, something true must lie beyond them and thus beyond our knowledge. We can’t know. We won’t know. Gödel’s results, known as the Incompleteness Theorems, are landmark achievements of 20th-century logic. Recent work in philosophy and mathematics has begun to illuminate the gap they opened, and with it something deeper about the foundations of knowledge itself. The story begins in the same city, but two centuries earlier. W hen Kant remembered a book by David Hume he’d read years earlier, he was woken from what he called his ‘dogmatic slumber’. Kant had believed that pure reasoning could teach us every absolute truth about the world, but Hume had asked a question that that stumped him: what reason do we actually have to believe that the future will resemble the past? We may have seen the Sun rise a thousand times, but how do we know it will rise tomorrow? The belief that the future will resemble the past is, Hume argued, merely a kind of habit. Kant couldn’t answer Hume’s challenge and was silenced for a decade. For Hume, proving a theorem was like unpacking a suitcase to find an outfit. Kant could not leave it there When Kant finally broke his silence with the Critique of Pure Reason (1781), he offered a radical answer. We can know the world only as it appears to our minds, and that is where our science sits; the noumenal world – the Ding an si
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-century-long-search-for-the-limits-to-knowledge

India had nearly a billion voters during the 2024 national elections. Now, over 130 million Indians, more than the population of countries such as Japan, Egypt and the Philippines, have been left off the draft voter rolls in an exercise called the Special Intensive Revision, or SIR. While election officers went to the homes of the powerful to verify their papers and bring them back onto the voter roll, ordinary Indians have been running from pillar to post to have their names counted. Sounam and her siblings don’t know what to do next. “Our brother was forced to prove his Indianness until his last breath.   FULL TEXT: It is almost four o’clock in the morning. As a woman in my late 40s, I have become prone to waking at this witching hour, as the early sunlight meets the night, sliding across the earth like a red tide. With the rest of my household asleep, and still half in and out of wakefulness myself, I take hold of my phone. With scarcely a thought, I switch from one app to another, scrolling through images and opinions and adverts for no discernible reason beyond habit. Later, I head for my office and open my laptop to a slew of half-completed documents and an inbox brimming with emails. My stress levels begin to nudge upwards. I have vague memories of what my professional life felt like before smartphones and video calls. I used to do my writing by hand. And I could concentrate for hours at a time, getting little upticks of arousal from lifting my head from the page, letting the hold on my pen loosen, and gazing at the life beyond my window, the light softening on my skin and eyes like snowfall on warm tarmac. Nowadays, my productive bursts come from caffeine and sugary or salty snacks, even if I try to pick the healthier ones, and from checking my emails or picking up my phone to update my inbox. By the end of the day, the sheer volume of work I expect myself to do, now that digital tools make so much possible, has kept me nailed to my seat. Another day has passed and the hope I’d had that I might have time for a run or a hike outside has been dashed again. I wish I could say that this is atypical. Yet, during the COVID-19 pandemic, I went from hours spent in nature and on physical activities to a passive, phone-addicted individual, meeting people first and sometimes only online. I am not alone. We have fallen into habits that are steadily deskilling the human body, eroding capacities honed by evolution to support our wellbeing as we move through the world. Some of these capacities are weakening through disuse: we have come to rely on our phones, for example, and now, increasingly, on AI, to rapidly do work that would once have required something more physical – like looking around, or asking someone, or going to find and consult a book. Reliance on GPS may weaken both our spatial attention and the fluency of our wayfinding. In surrendering to a quick technological fix, we are, moment by moment, doing less sensing, navigating, remembering, walking, choosing, and attending.   https://aeon.co/essays/the-more-we-rely-on-tech-the-more-we-deskill-our-bodies

  FULL TEXT: Until a decade ago, the road leading to the College of Education in Yemen’s southern port city of al-Mukalla was crowded with students every morning. Inside the campus, classrooms were packed with male and female students, while the corridors bustled with latecomers rushing to their lectures. Groups of students gathered under trees to prepare for classes, while others wandered the corridors after being barred from entering classrooms because they had arrived late. During breaks, long queues of hungry and thirsty students formed outside the college cafeteria, where they crowded around a small serving window, hoping to buy a bottle of water or a snack before returning to class. The dormitories, located on the same campus, were just as lively in the afternoons and evenings. Some students, wearing white undershirts, sat outside their rooms studying in the dusty courtyard. Others played soccer or slept in the open air to escape the stifling heat and humidity inside their rooms. In the evenings, groups of students headed downtown in search of entertainment or a decent meal, tired of the food served at the college. During the first semester of their final year at college, hundreds of aspiring teachers were assigned to local schools, where they spent an entire semester teaching, putting into practice what they had learned and preparing for their future careers. The large number of trainee teachers helped fill staffing shortages at local schools, while also allowing regular teachers to take a break from their teaching duties. Visiting recently, I was struck by the starkly contrasting scenes at the college. The classrooms were empty, the corridors silent, and the dormitories were gathering dust, with their windows and doors either deteriorating or missing altogether. I was told that similar scenes are unfolding at colleges of education and other teacher training institutions across Yemen, including in Aden, Taiz, Shabwa, al-Mahra and Hadramawt. Many have been forced to phase out departments because enrollment has plummeted, from hundreds of students more than a decade ago to zero in some departments today. * Is everything conscious?
  Aeon | 12 Oct 10:00 UTC
  If the question makes no sense to you, try spending some time with the principle of sufficient reason - by Sam Kimpton-Nye Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: When an explanation makes a fact intelligible, the mystery goes out of it. We understand it so well that we can hardly conceive of things being otherwise. Consider the simple mathematical fact that one plus one equals two. Once we understand elementary arithmetic, we no longer feel the urge to ask why one plus one equals two. That one plus one equals two strikes us as not merely true but unavoidable; we cannot even coherently imagine its falsity. Much of philosophy aims to bring other fundamental features of reality into a similar light, to make them appear not arbitrary but inevitable and, thus, intelligible. This aspiration that philosophy render the world intelligible is best expressed by the German polymath Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz and the Dutch philosopher Baruch Spinoza . According to Leibniz: ‘we can find no true or existent fact … without there being a sufficient reason why it is thus and not otherwise.’ And, as Spinoza wrote: ‘For each thing there must be assigned a cause or reason, both for its existence and for its nonexistence.’ What’s being expressed here, in the words of the US philosopher Michael Della Rocca, is the desire ‘to make the world and our place in it intelligible’. To grasp the kinds of reasons or explanations sought by Leibniz and Spinoza ‘is the perennial hope and promise of philosophy’. The demand for intelligibility can lead to some extraordinary places. For Leibniz, the demand led him to propose that reality is ultimately made up of a multiplicity of monads: indivisible, infinitesimally small units of substance, each of which is a causally isolated, self-contained universe. Spinoza’s resolute commitment to universal intelligibility led him to conclude that reality is, instead, a single infinite substance that he called God or nature. As we will see, the demand for intelligibility can also lead into the strange and fascinating world of panpsychism. Panpsychism is an increasingly popular view about consciousness. You are conscious, you feel pleasures, pains and all manner of other experiences. At every waking moment, and even when you dream, there is something that it is like to be you. The same goes for other humans and animals, and this accounts for moral status: thumping a fist on a desk in frustration is morally permissible in a way that hitting a conscious person or animal is not. Desks, chairs, rocks and so on are not conscious – and are therefore without moral status – whereas humans, bonobos, dogs and so on are conscio
  https://aeon.co/essays/does-the-principle-of-sufficient-reason-make-panpsychism-true

* A Rupture in the History of Consciousness
  New Lines | 09 Oct 10:33 UTC
  Over the centuries, layers of interpretation have mediated our experience and separated the self from the world, but glimpses of an older mode of consciousness can still be found. Even modern literature that is transfixed by the self and immersed in its workings can fleetingly reveal the older intimacy. The post A Rupture in the History o...
  FULL TEXT: In sixth-century Arabia, a man wakes up at night and begins to saddle his she-camel, something he must have done countless times, and she’s tired of it. The man is al-Muthaqqib al-Abdi, a pre-Islamic Arab poet whose three surviving poems have been acclaimed by classical and modern critics alike. In this poem, we glimpse a rare form of consciousness, in which the borders between man and camel almost disappear. In the poet’s mind, they coexist within a shared matrix of existence that doesn’t split the world into subject and object, into the human being standing here and nature out there. Al-Abdi says in his poem (my translation is informed in part by the rendering of the late-19th- and early-20th-century scholar C. J. Lyall): When I rise at night to saddle her, she sighs the sigh of a sorrowing man. As I lay out the girth for her, she says: “Is this forever to be his way, and mine? Must all of life be stopping and setting out again? Will he never spare me or keep me from harm?” To me, the ingenuity of these lines lies in their directness, honesty and lack of imposed poetic devices, despite the highly structured verse. The poet, the camel and the journey itself share a seamless order of being while also remaining distinct from one another. Their knowledge of each other is so intimate that he reads her groan as a sigh that reveals her suffering to him. Yet move he must, as the harsh realities of the desert demand. The coming of Islam ruptured that shared matrix and the worldview that came with it, calling on people to take stock of the world and see in it signs pointing to the one God. But this new way of seeing never fully destroyed the earlier mode of perception that had been present during the Jahiliyya — the name given to the pre-Islamic period of Arabian history, literally meaning the “age of ignorance.” Some Bedouin herders still form close attachments to camels they know by name, a present-day trace of that older intimacy, similar to the way that many in the Arab world and beyond bond with their horses; I saw some of these interactions firsthand when I lived in the UAE. Such intimacy could persist even as abstract concepts and dissecting principles gained authority, pulling humans away from their surroundings and setting them at a distance. Greek philosophers also distanced us from that older state of consciousness, attempting to unearth principles that lie beneath appearances. * Barbara Ward’s vision
  Aeon | 09 Oct 10:00 UTC
  She was among the first to argue that prosperity, equality and the protection of nature are inseparable planetary goals - by Or Rosenboim Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Barbara Ward at the UN Conference on the Human Environment in Stockholm, Sweden, in 1972. Courtesy UN Photo In 1957, the economist and journalist Barbara Ward gave the first of the Aggrey-Fraser-Guggisberg Memorial Lectures at the University College of Ghana. Shortly after Kwame Nkrumah led the British colony of the Gold Coast towards self-government, he invited Ward to address the independent country’s first cohort of students. Ward was well acquainted with the local political landscape because she had spent the previous four years living in the Gold Coast, witnessing its political transformation. As the world’s eyes were set on Ghana, the first sub-Saharan colony to achieve independence from European rule, Ward was determined to provide her audience with the knowledge needed to govern their newly founded country. Her lectures, titled Five Ideas that Change the World , developed a world-historical narrative of the supposed human progress from nationalism to colonialism, industrialism, communism, and finally to internationalism, the highest point of political order. Ghana’s peaceful democratic transition showed that African colonies were ready to govern themselves, weakening European arguments about the need for prolonged imperial guidance. The country soon became a model for new African statehood based on postcolonial sovereignty, inspiring other colonies on the continent to join the decolonisation process, which culminated in the ‘Year of Africa’ in 1960. The charismatic Nkrumah, who ruled Ghana from 1957 to 1966, became an international player in Cold War diplomacy by promoting pan-Africanist visions of post-imperial unity. He wanted to show the world that Africans were no longer imperial subjects but important and influential members of the global political community. While Nkrumah was a vociferous opponent of colonialism, Ward uncritically appreciated the civilisational and technical legacies of empire. Yet in her lectures, she supported Nkrumah’s international political aspirations by suggesting that economic development, international cooperation, and even world peace were interrelated problems that warranted a planetary solution. B arbara Ward was a remarkable thinker, a visionary who imagined a ‘planetary’ political, economic and environmental justice based on both material and spiritual principles. She was an unusual figure in the Cold War political sphere, a charismatic public intellectual who advised world leaders and the United Nations at a ti
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-we-should-heed-barbara-wards-vision-of-planetary-justice
